--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Understanding the mission:**
- Before switch time: score increases for every STATIONARY robot → robots should MOVE before switch
- After switch time: score increases for every MOVING robot → robots should STOP after switch
- Switch time is unknown (400-600 steps), signaled by a GREEN wall lighting up
- The green light is the key signal: robots should be moving before they see green, then stop when they see green

**Analysis of current best:**
- Score 9221.71: Just `--nstates 1 --s0 1` (Stop always) — this is surprisingly good because the second phase penalty (moving) is avoided entirely, but the first phase penalty (stationary) is fully incurred. With 18 robots and ~500 steps before switch + 700 steps after, stopping always gives: 18×500 (before) + 0×700 (after) = 9000 expected, which matches well.

**Ideal strategy:**
- Move before green light appears → 0 penalty in phase 1
- Stop when green light appears → 0 penalty in phase 2
- Key: detect green (color 2 in ARGoS = green? Need to check: clr values 1-6 correspond to colors)

Looking at the FSMs: `--clr 2` appears in several machines associated with green detection. The wall lights up GREEN, which should be detectable via condition C=7 (perceives a given colour) with `--l<I>x<K> 2` (assuming color 2 = green).

**Target FSM:**
- State 0: Exploration (moving) → transition to Stop when green light is seen (C=7, color 2)
- State 1: Stop → stay stopped

This should score near-optimally: ~0 in phase 1 (moving) + ~0 in phase 2 (stopped after green detected).

**Population plan:**
1. Champion (elitism): Stop only
2-6: Exploitation of best strategy (move → stop on green)
7-14: Exploration variants

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (Score: 6474.68):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=50) → transitions to State 1 (Stop) when green detected (C=7, color 2, p=0.9) OR randomly with p=0.01
- State 1: Stop → transitions back to State 0 randomly (p=0.005) OR when green detected (p=0.1) — wait, the second transition goes to state 1 again? No: `--n1x1 1` with 3 states means destination index 1 among "others" from state 1. Others from state 1 are [0, 2], so D=1 targets state 2.
- State 1: Stop → goes to State 2 (GoToColor green) when green seen (p=0.1)
- State 2: GoToColor green → goes to State 1 (Stop) when green seen (p=0.95)

So the logic is: Explore → see green → Stop. If stopped and see green again, go toward green. Near green → stop again. This is clever — it both detects the green signal and moves toward it for better detection, then stops.

**Key improvements to try:**
1. The random transition from state 0 (p=0.01) causes some premature stopping in phase 1 — try removing or reducing it
2. The transition from stop back to explore (p=0.005) is very low — good
3. Try increasing green detection probability (0.9 → 0.95, 0.99)
4. Try having state 1 not go to state 2 at all (just stop permanently after green)
5. Try different rwm values for exploration

**Exploitation variants:**
- Increase green detection threshold in state 0 (0.9 → 0.95, 0.99)
- Remove/reduce random stop transition (0.01 → 0.001)
- Adjust state 1 green detection probability (0.1 → higher or lower)
- Try 2-state version: Explore → Stop on green, no going back

**Exploration:**
- 4-state machines with more nuanced behavior
- Using neighbor counts to help detect aggregation near the green wall

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (Score: 6355.13):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=70, higher turning rate) → go to Stop (state 1) if green seen (p=0.9) OR randomly (p=0.01)
- State 1: Stop → go back to Explore (state 0) with p=0.005 OR go to GoToColor green (state 2) if green seen (p=0.05)
- State 2: GoToColor green → go to Stop (state 1) if green seen (p=0.99)

**Key observations:**
- rwm=70 beats rwm=50 and rwm=30 — higher turning rate helps exploration
- The p=0.05 transition from Stop→GoToColor is lower than p=0.1 in runner-up, suggesting less time in GoToColor is better
- State 2 (GoToColor) with p=0.99 for stopping is very aggressive at stopping once near green
- The small random transition (p=0.01) from explore to stop seems harmful but small enough to not matter much
- The 3-state structure with GoToColor as intermediary is consistently winning

**Exploitation strategy:**
- Try rwm=80, 90, 100 (push higher turning rate)
- Try reducing p0x1 (random stop from explore) to 0.005 or 0.002
- Try p1x1 (stop→GoToColor on green) at 0.02, 0.03, 0.07
- Try p0x0 (explore→stop on green) at 0.85, 0.95
- Add neighbor-count transitions to detect aggregation near wall

**Exploration:**
- 4-state machine with an additional "approach" state
- Variant where state 0 also has neighbor detection
- Variant with different GoToColor approach

- State 0: Exploration (rwm=70) → Stop (state 1) if green seen (p=0.9) OR randomly (p=0.01) OR if >6 neighbors (threshold=6, steepness=10)
- State 1: Stop → Explore (state 0) randomly (p=0.005) OR GoToColor green (state 2) if green seen (p=0.05)

1. The neighbor-count transition (C=3, p=6, w=10) in state 0 adds value — helps when robots cluster near a wall before green appears? 5. Reducing p0x1 below 0.005 hurts (score 7351); keeping at 0.01 seems right
6. p1x1=0.05 (stop → GoToColor on green) is consistently good; lower values better than higher

--- POPULATION PROPOSEE POUR CE ROUND ---
## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (Score: 5677.81):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=70) → Stop if green seen (p=0.9) OR randomly (p=0.01) OR if >4 neighbors (threshold=4, steepness=10)
- State 1: Stop → Explore randomly (p=0.005) OR GoToColor green if green seen (p=0.05)
- State 2: GoToColor green → Stop if green seen (p=0.99)

**Key insights from this round:**
1. Neighbor threshold=4 (steepness=10) beats threshold=5,6,7,8 — lower threshold means robots stop earlier when they cluster near the green wall
2. rwm=70 beats rwm=75 and others
3. p1x1=0.05 (stop→GoToColor on green) is best; p=0.02 is worse
4. Adding a 4th transition to state 0 (duplicate green detection) slightly helps (score 6287)
5. The 3-state neighbor-based structure is converging

**What to try next:**
- Neighbor threshold=3 (even lower) with steepness=10, 8, 15
- Neighbor threshold=4 with steepness=8, 12, 15, 20
- Combine neighbor threshold with 4-state structure
- Try threshold=2 (very aggressive early stopping)
- Vary p0x1 (random stop): try 0.008, 0.015
- Try p0x0=0.9 with threshold=4 but different steepness
- Try having state 2 also have a random fallback to stop

**Avoid:** threshold=6 with steepness=10, p1x1=0.02, extra neighbor condition in state 1

## Reasoning

**Champion analysis (Score: 5285.76):**
- State 0: Exploration (rwm=70) → Stop if green seen (p=0.9) OR randomly (p=0.01) OR if ≥2 neighbors (threshold=2, steepness=10)
- State 1: Stop → Explore randomly (p=0.005) OR GoToColor green if green seen (p=0.05)
- State 2: GoToColor green → Stop if green seen (p=0.99)

**Key insights:**
1. **Neighbor threshold=2 is the winner** — very low threshold means robots stop as soon as they encounter even one other robot nearby. This is a strong proxy for "being near a wall/cluster" which correlates with being in a good stopping position.
2. The steepness=10 is working well for threshold=2
3. p0x1=0.01 random stop is the sweet spot (0.015 also works, 0.008 hurts)
4. p1x1=0.05 (stop→GoToColor) is best
5. 4-state machines and duplicate green transitions don't help

**What to try next:**
- Neighbor threshold=1 (most aggressive — stop if ANY neighbor nearby)
- Threshold=2 with steepness=8, 12, 15, 20 (fine-tune the sigmoid)
- Threshold=2 with p0x1=0.012, 0.013 (between 0.01 and 0.015)
- Threshold=3 with lower steepness (8) — since threshold=3 with steepness=8 was 4th best
- Try p0x0=0.85, 0.88 (slight reduction in green detection)
- p1x0=0.004, 0.006 (small variation around 0.005)
- Try neighbor threshold=2 with steepness=6 (even softer sigmoid)

**Avoid:** threshold=4 with steepness=10, p1x0=0.003, 4-state structures, duplicate transitions

## Reasoning

**Current state of convergence:**
The structure is very stable: 3 states, rwm=70, threshold=2, steepness=8-10, p0x0=0.88-0.9, p0x1=0.01, p1x0=0.004-0.006, p1x1=0.05, p2x0=0.99.

**Key findings from round 7:**
1. Threshold=1 is worse (score 5442) — threshold=2 is the sweet spot
2. Steepness=8 ties with steepness=10 (both ~5357)
3. p1x0=0.006 slightly helps (5317 vs 5285) — but champion is still 5285
4. p0x0=0.88 is slightly worse (5345 vs 5285)
5. p1x1=0.04 hurts (5452)
6. p0x1=0.012 hurts (5416)

**Fine-tuning directions:**
- p1x0: try 0.005, 0.006, 0.007 (narrow range)
- steepness: try 9, 10, 11, 12 (fine-grained around 8-10)
- p0x0: try 0.90, 0.91, 0.92 (check slightly above)
- p1x1: try 0.05, 0.055, 0.06 (slightly above)
- p2x0: try 0.995, 1.0 — wait, max is 1.0 but let's try 0.995
- Try combining best sub-parameters: p1x0=0.006, steepness=8
- Also try p0x1=0.009, 0.011 (near 0.01)

**Exploration:** Given convergence is deep, try one or two genuinely different ideas:
- What if state 1 (Stop) has a C=3 neighbor condition to go back to exploring when isolated?
- What if state 2 has a fallback random stop?

## Reasoning

**Final round analysis:**
The champion (5285.76) has held for 3 rounds. This is the last round. Key findings:

1. **p1x1=0.052** gives 5364 (better than 0.048=5495, 0.05=5285 champion)
2. **rwm=72** gives 5378 (better than rwm=68=5462)
3. **p0x0=0.895** gives 5392 (close but worse)
4. **p1x0=0.0045** gives 5400 (worse than 0.005)
5. **p2x0=0.985** hurts (5456), 0.99 is optimal
6. **p1x1=0.048** hurts (5495), 0.05 is better than 0.048 but 0.052 is better

**For the final round, focus on:**
- Combinations of the best sub-parameters: rwm=72 + p1x1=0.052
- rwm=71, 73 (near 72)
- p1x1=0.051, 0.053, 0.054 (near 0.052)
- Combining rwm=72 with p1x1=0.052
- Try p0x0=0.9 with all best combinations
- One or two genuine explorations as insurance

**Note:** All micro-variations are very close. The champion score of 5285 is likely near-optimal for this structure. The final push is to find marginally better parameter combinations.

################################################################################
# PROMPT SYSTEME
################################################################################

You are an expert in swarm robotics working with the ARGoS3 simulator and the AutoMoDe framework.
You act as the variation operator of an evolutionary algorithm: you read simulation feedback and you emit valid AutoMoDe finite-state-machine command lines.

# 1. THE MISSION
The score is MINIMISED.

Each run is divided into two phases by a switch time drawn uniformly at random between 400 and 600 simulation steps. The robots are not told when it occurs.

Before the switch time, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is stationary. From the switch time onwards, at every step, the score increases by 1 for every robot that is moving. A robot counts as moving if it travelled more than 0.0005 m since the previous step.

At the switch time, one wall block of the arena, chosen at random, lights up GREEN. The floor of the arena is uniformly GRAY.

# 2. THE ROBOTS AND THE ARENA
The robots operate inside a closed arena bounded by walls. 18 e-puck robots start scattered around the centre of the arena, with random positions and orientations that change at every run. Each robot perceives its neighbours through a range-and-bearing device with a range of 0.58 m: neighbour-count conditions only count robots closer than that. A run lasts 1200 simulation steps (120 s at 10 steps per second).

# 3. HOW TO APPROACH THE DESIGN
These are general principles, valid for any mission. They are not a solution to this one.
- Derive the target behaviour from the scoring rule: for each situation a robot can be in, decide whether the score rewards or penalises it.
- Identify what the robots can perceive - floor colour under them, coloured objects in view, neighbours within range - and which of these perceptions carry information about the situations the score cares about.
- Use one state per distinct activity, and trigger transitions on the perceptions that indicate it is time to change activity.

# 4. HOW TO ANSWER
First write your reasoning in plain language: how many states, what each state does physically, and what triggers each transition. Two or three sentences per machine is enough.
Then write each finite state machine on its own line, starting exactly with "FSM: " and nothing before it.

# 5. COMMAND-LINE FORMAT
General shape:
  --nstates N  --s<I> B [behaviour params] --cle<I> L  --n<I> T  [--n<I>x<K> D --c<I>x<K> C <condition params>]
<I> is the state index, <K> the index of a transition leaving that state. Both start at 0.

## A. Global
* `--nstates N` : number of states, integer in [1..4].

## B. Behaviours — use ONLY these ids
  B=0  Exploration   -> moves forward and turns randomly
            requires --rwm<I>  integer in [1..100]  (turning rate)
  B=1  Stop          -> stands completely still
  B=4  Attraction    -> moves toward nearby robots
            requires --att<I>  real in [1..5]  (attraction gain)
  B=5  Repulsion     -> moves away from nearby robots
            requires --rep<I>  real in [1..5]  (repulsion gain)
  B=8  GoToColor     -> moves toward the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)
  B=9  GoAwayColor   -> moves away from the nearest object of a target colour
            requires --vel<I>  fixed value 1.0  (speed)
            requires --clr<I>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (target colour)

Every state also takes an LED colour:
* `--cle<I> L` : one of {0,4,5,6}  (0=off, 1=red, 2=green, 3=blue, 4=yellow, 5=magenta, 6=cyan)

## C. Transitions
* `--n<I> T` : number of transitions leaving state <I>, integer in [1..4].
  Up to 4 transitions may leave the same state, each with a different condition type. A state that reacts to several different situations is often stronger than a state with one transition, and random sampling produces that variety by default.
* `--n<I>x<K> D` : destination, indexed among the OTHER states only.
  With 3 states, leaving state 1 the others are [0, 2]: D=0 targets state 0, D=1 targets state 2.
  With 2 states, leaving state 0 the only other state is state 1, so D must be 0.
* `--c<I>x<K> C` : condition type — use ONLY these ids
  C=0  floor under the robot is BLACK
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=1  floor under the robot is GRAY
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=2  floor under the robot is WHITE
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=3  number of neighbours ABOVE a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=4  number of neighbours BELOW a threshold
            requires --p<I>x<K>  integer in [1..10]  (threshold: an INTEGER count of neighbours, not a probability)
            requires --w<I>x<K>  real in [0..20]  (steepness of the sigmoid)
  C=5  fires with a fixed probability at every step
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])
  C=7  the robot PERCEIVES a given colour with its camera
            requires --l<I>x<K>  one of {1,2,3,4,5,6}  (colour to look for)
            requires --p<I>x<K>  real in [0..1]  (probability, real in [0,1])

# 6. HARD RULES
1. Integers stay integers; real-valued parameters always carry a decimal point (1.0, not 1).
2. Every state that exists must declare `--n<I>`, and every declared transition must carry its destination, its condition type and that condition's parameters. A missing parameter crashes the simulator.
3. `--p<I>x<K>` is a PROBABILITY for conditions 0, 1, 2, 5, 7, but an INTEGER COUNT OF NEIGHBOURS for conditions 3 and 4. Never write 0.8 where a neighbour count is expected.
4. Never point a transition at its own state.

# 7. YOU ARE THE EVOLUTIONARY OPERATOR
This is a multi-round conversation on one real ARGoS3 optimisation run. The numerical optimiser is restarted from scratch at the beginning of every round, so exploration and exploitation are entirely up to you.

1. SURVIVAL (elitism): copy the current best-ever machine verbatim in your answer so its lineage is not lost.
2. EXPLOITATION: spend about half of your machines on small edits of the best performers — shift a probability, a threshold or a gain by roughly ten percent, and keep the topology.
3. EXPLORATION: spend the rest on genuinely different topologies or module combinations, to escape local optima.
4. RICHNESS: do not let every state settle on a single transition of the same condition type. Include candidates with two to four transitions per state mixing different condition types.
